## Appendix C History of modeling e ff orts for specific sources 

In this section we give a source-by-source overview of modeling attempts for individual accreting X-ray pulsars. Table C3 lists the sources addressed here, for further information on the profiles and their variations of these sources see also Table B2. For each source we provide a brief overview of the information derived by the modeling e ff orts over the course of time in order to demonstrate the spread in results coming from di ff erent approaches and assumptions. The sources are ordered by the dates in which they were detected as pulsating X-ray sources, which often coincides with their original detection but in some cases was significantly later. Table C3 lists the sources sorted by right ascension. 

Cen X-3 

This eclipsing X-ray binary in a tight orbit around its supergiant companion was the first discovered X-ray pulsar (Giacconi et al. 1971a). It has a spin period of about 4.9 s. Daishido (1975) developed a description of beamed X-ray emission due to anisotropic Thomson scattering of X-rays from hot spots in strong B-fields and oblique rotators. Applying this to the early Uhuru profiles (Schreier et al. 1972), he derived values for the intensity of the surface B-field (8 × 10 11 G), the inclination of the spin axis (67 ◦), and magnetic obliquity (67 ◦). Wang and Welter (1981) found that the best match to the observed profile within their geometrical description of pulse profiles was for intersecting fan beams. Leahy (1990) found as best fit for Cen X-3 hollow polar caps, or broad rings ∼22 ◦ wide with an inner opening angle of ∼18 ◦. The preferred solution of Leahy (1991) had two broad, hollow polar caps with relatively small gaps around the magnetic axis and a large o ff set angle of ∼97 ◦ between the two hot spots. 92 Ri ff ert et al. (1993) studied the influence of relativistic light deflection near the surface of the neutron star and found important qualitative di ff erences to Leahy (1991). An example solution for Cen X-3 had two polar caps with an opening angle of 10 ◦ each, small angles between observer and rotation axis and between the rotation and magnetic axes, and an o ff set of only ∼16 ◦ from directly opposed polar caps. Kraus et al. (1996) applied the method of Kraus et al. (1995) to a range of pulse profiles obtained at di ff erent times for Cen X-3. They found that the asymmetry of the pulse profile could be explained assuming two symmetrical emission regions, combining pencil and fan beam emission, which are displaced from antipodal positions by ∼10 ◦. The two regions contribute quite di ff erently to the overall profile. After a long pause in modeling of this source, Saatho ff et al. (2024) applied the method of Saatho ff (2023) using the pulse-to-pulse variability in the observed flux to RXTE light curves of Cen X-3. They found two distinct single-pole pulse profiles of approximately equal amplitude and width where both components are asymmetric and one reflected in phase to the other. These results are in marked contrast with the basic assumption and results of Kraus et al. (1996). Using the Polestar model, Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 74 ◦.0 ± 1◦.2 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 70 ◦.2 ± 2◦.7 for the inclination of the orbit. Polarization analysis is possible only if the spin axis is fixed to the orbital inclination (Tsygankov et al. 2022). The magnetic obliquity is found 16 ◦.4 ± 1◦.3. The strong changes in the behavior of the polarization angle with phase (Zhao et al. 2026, see also Sect. 5.2) and energy cast doubt on the interpretation of these simple RVM fits, although Tsygankov et al. (2022) remark that the polarization behavior resembles one of the two components found by Kraus et al. (1996) in their pulse profile decomposition. 

GX 1 +4

The source was detected by Lewin et al. (1971) during a balloon flight with indications for periodic flux changes with a period of about 2.3 minutes (138 s). In the years after discovery an unusually strong spin-up of ∼20% within a decade was seen. After years of quiescence during the 1980s the source became brighter again, now with a similarly strong spin down ( ∼50% pulse period increase over ∼25 years, see Gonz´ alez-Gal´ an et al. 2012, and references therein). The optical counterpart is the M6III star V2116 Oph, with Porb ∼ 1161 d (Hinkle et al. 2006, and references therein). The pulse profile shape for this source is extremely variable, with very di ff erent shapes observed at di ff erent times and flux levels (e.g., Manchanda 1989; Greenhill et al. 1998; Jaisawal et al. 2018b). This strong variability with time and flux (see also Table B2) would in principle provide additional constraints for modeling, since one would expect to have a (nearly) stable and B-field configuration on the surface of the neutron star and field strength. On the other hand, existing e ff orts started from specific sets of profiles. Because of the visual similarity between a pulse of GX 1 +4 shown by Becker et al. (1976) and the profile of Her X-1, Mitrofanov and Tsygan (1978) concluded that accretion would be mainly on a single magnetic pole. 93 Leahy (1990) found as best fit a narrow polar ring of ∼4◦ width, at ∼36 ◦ from the magnetic axis. The solutions found by Leahy (1991) were more varied, but still preferred two rings a few degrees width with at most a small o ff set of ∼6◦ from opposition. David et al. (1998) described pulse profiles observed at di ff erent times by SIGMA in the 40–77 keV energy range. Following Basko and Sunyaev (1976), the symmetrized pulse profiles were modeled by hollow, thin walled, cylinders aligned with the magnetic dipole axis. Gravitational light bending was not considered. Based on the di ff erent observed pulse profile shapes, David et al. derived varying accretion column heights between 0 .4 ± 0.4 and (6 ± 2) × 10 −3 RNS for angles between rotation axis and B-field and rotation axis and line of sight of ∼84 ◦ or ∼5◦ – as in most descriptions, it is not possible to say which value corresponds to which of these two angles. Galloway and Wu (2001) describe a model with two homogeneous, axisymmetric, cyclin-drical emission regions, where the emission is beamed at an angle >90 ◦ with respect to the column axis. This model gave a qualitative explanation for the sharp primary minimum usually observed, with variations depending on the inclination between magnetic axis and observer and the angle between magnetic and rotation axis. They also noted that their model would produce quite symmetric profiles and that the observed asymmetry would require additional mechanisms, such as a variation in density across the accretion column. 

Her X-1 

This iconic X-ray pulsar with a spin period of 1.24 s and an orbital period of 1.7 d was discovered by Tananbaum et al. (1972). The pulse profile shows variations on all timescales from super-orbital (Sect. 5.4) to short-term. See, for example, Staubert et al. (2013), Brumback et al. (2021), and Table B2. Using the same model and analysis approach as that used for Cen X-3, Daishido (1975) derived the surface B-field intensity (8 ×10 11 G), inclination of the spin axis (45 ◦), and magnetic obliquity (81 ◦). Bisnovatyi-Kogan et al. (1975) attempted to explain the variations between the early pulse profile observations (Giacconi et al. 1971a; Doxsey et al. 1973; Holt et al. 1974) by assuming a “cup-shaped” radiation pattern at both poles and ascribing the main pulse to one pole and the interpulse to the other. They noted that for more standard patterns a displacement of the magnetic axis from the center of rotation or di ff erent accretion rates at the poles would be required. Mitrofanov and Tsygan (1978) explained the clear dominance of the main pulse with respect to the interpulse by accretion only to a single magnetic pole, since similar accretion to both poles should lead to two similar pulses. Yahel (1980a) compared results of detailed radiative transport calculations of X-ray pulsar models (Yahel 1980b) to the spectra and light curves of Her X-1. They concluded that the radiation would be emitted from the column in an asymmetric fan beam pattern with a beam center on average 500–1000 m above the surface. Nagel (1981b) presented results of radiative transfer calculations for radiating slabs and columns of strongly magnetized plasma and compared these qualitatively with observed profiles. Their best match was for a thin slab of 2 m height emitting a pencil beam pattern. According to their own discussion this solution was not physical, as it would have required a too large radiation area, but other configurations were clashing with other observed properties. 94 The asymmetric shape of the main pulse could also not be replicated in their intrinsically symmetrical model. Wang and Welter (1981) described the observed pulse profiles with asymmetric, inter-secting fan beam patterns. Time variations in the X-ray emission were considered to come either from changes in the geometry of the accretion flow or Thomson-opaque plasma in the equatorial direction. Leahy (1990) fitted the pulse profile by a broad polar ring with an inner and outer angle of 

∼18 ◦ and ∼40 ◦, respectively, a similar solution as the one for Cen X-3. Leahy (1991) found the two polar rings o ff set from a perfect dipole by ∼5◦ or ∼11 ◦ in the two solutions presented. The derived polar rings ranged from <1◦ to ∼13 ◦ width with opening angles of ∼21 ◦ to ∼34 ◦.Burnard et al. (1991) presented results from their description of polar caps with accretion mounds to match the gross features (total luminosity, polar cap area, and B-field strength) of Her X-1, but noted that the derived curves did not capture all the features of the observed data, indicating that the accretion mound was more complex. Panchenko and Postnov (1994) assumed a complex B-field structure, approximated by assuming three distinct zones on the neutron star surface: two magnetic poles and a circle-like area around the pole with lower B-field strength. General relativistic e ff ects were not taken into account. Blum and Kraus (2000) applied the method of Kraus et al. (1995) to a total of 148 pulse profiles from 20 di ff erent observations. They found all data compatible with the assumption of a slightly distorted magnetic dipole field as sole cause of the asymmetry of the observed pulse profiles and evidence that the emission from both poles was equal. They derived an angle 

< 20 ◦ between the rotation axis and the local magnetic axis and an o ff set angle < 5◦ from the antipodal position, while the beam pattern structures indicated both pencil- and fan-beam emission. Di ff erent attenuation of the radiation from the poles was invoked to explain the variations of pulse profiles at di ff erent phases of the 35 d cycle (Sect. 5.4). Scott et al. (2000) developed an elaborate phenomenological model for the cyclical pulse profile variations including coaxial pencil and fan beam emission, with the pencil beam emanating from the polar cap and the fan beam emanating from a high column and being focused backwards around the neutron star towards the observer. In this model the magnetic axis was significantly inclined by 48 ◦ with respect to the rotation axis. The variations would be caused by occultation of the pulse-emitting region by the tilted, inner edge of a precessing accretion disk. Based on the code described for hollow columns by Leahy (2003), but also allowing for filled cones, Leahy (2004a) described the pulse profile and its changes by a combination of a pencil beam from the near pole and a fan beam from the far pole, emitted from the top and sides of relatively flat filled cones (cone half-angle: 6 ◦, height: 0 .025 RNS ) at magnetic poles off set by ∼7◦ from opposition. Leahy (2004b) expanded this work, finding a new solution (cone half-angle: 15 ◦, height: 0 .075 RNS ) with one emission region at ∼50 ◦ and the other at 

∼30 ◦ from the poles of the rotation axis, also deriving limits on the mass-radius relation for the neutron star in Her X-1. Postnov et al. (2013) invoked free precession of the neutron star and a complex B-field structure as the drivers behind the observed pulse profile changes. Guided by the measured profiles, they elaborated a complex geometrical model with one pole close to the axis of inertia and the other at ∼30 ◦ from this, multiple arc segments forming rings around these poles plus 95 some additional arcs. Light bending was not taken into account with the justification that most of the emission in this model was escaping close to normal to the surface. Based on the Polestar model, Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 55 ◦.0 ± 1◦.1for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 80 ◦.5 ± 3◦.8 for the inclination of the orbit (a magnetic obliquity of about 25 deg). Gibson and Becker (2026) simulated the formation of the pulse profile using a model with dual conical accretion columns. The continuum emission from each column was computed using the Becker and Wol ff (2022) model, and the angular distribution of the radiation (the beaming pattern) was determined as part of the fitting process. The geometrical results obtained are similar to those found for this source by Leahy (1991). IXPE observations show an average 2–7 keV degree of polarization of 8 .6 ± 0.5% (Doroshenko et al. 2022). In phase resolved spectroscopy the PD shows complex behavior, but the polarization angle oscillates in a sinusoidal way with an amplitude of about 20 ◦ that can be well modeled with the RVM, yielding a magnetic obliquity of 12 ◦.1 ± 3◦.7 and an inclination of 

i = 96 +38  

> −41

deg (Doroshenko et al. 2022). Heyl et al. (2024) and Zhao et al. (2024) build on this work by analyzing all observations of Her X-1 with IXPE, which were taken at di ff erent phases of the 35 d cycle. The degree of polarization in the main on is found to be compatible with that of Doroshenko et al. (2022), in the short on it is higher (around 18%). RVM modeling of these observations indicates large changes in the magnetic obliquity, inclination, and position angle and is speculated by these authors to be compatible with free precession of the neutron star. which had been earlier suspected from its long-term variability and pulse profile analysis (Staubert et al. 2009; Postnov et al. 2013; Kolesnikov et al. 2022). 

Cep X-4 

Detected with OSO-7 by Ulmer et al. (1973) as a transient X-ray source, 66.2 s pulsations were first noted in Ginga observations of an outburst in 1988 (Makino and GINGA Team 1988b). The pulse profile was published by Nagase (1989, using the source name GS 2137 +57). A more complete characterisation of this Be X-ray pulsar was provided by Koyama et al. (1991), confirming the connection to Cep X-4. Leahy (1991) included this source in his model e ff orts as GS 2137 +57. The preferred solution had narrow polar rings with widths of ∼1◦ and ∼4◦ at >45 ◦ from the magnetic poles, which were o ff set by ∼22 ◦.Using the Polestar model, Laycock et al. (2025) derived a low inclination of 12 ◦.3 ± 0◦.8 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight. The orbital inclination is unknown. 

1A 0535 +262 

X-ray pulsations with a period of 104 s of this transient Be X-ray binary ( Porb = 111 .1 d) were reported by Rosenberg et al. (1975). From the same outburst, Bradt et al. (1976) published detailed pulse profiles obtained with SAS-3 in six energy bands between 1.2 and 40 keV, while Ricker et al. (1976) presented pulse profiles up to ∼100 keV from a balloon-borne telescope. Mitrofanov and Tsygan (1978) explained these broadband pulse profiles with two very broad hot spots emitting almost isotropically, but separated by ∼60 ◦ rather than antipodal. Mitrofanov and Tsygan emphasized that “gravitational inflection” of the X-rays has to be taken into account and invoked one curved column to explain the uneven number of peaks observed in the soft band. 96 In order to explain the change from a multi-peaked pulse profile at lower to a double-peaked shape at higher energies, Kanno (1980) proposed for this source (and Vela X-1, see below) that the X-rays are emitted from hot spots at the magnetic poles into an optically thick neutron star atmosphere where the opacity is dominated by Thomson scattering by strongly magnetized but only weakly dispersive electrons. The appearance of the complex pattern would then depend on the relative directions of the spin axis, magnetic axis, and line of sight. In contrast, Wang and Welter (1981) suggested a fan-beam pattern to explain the broad plateau profile observed at hard X-rays. Based on the 9–18 keV pulse profile (Wang and Welter 1981; Bradt et al. 1976), Leahy (1990) derived ∼10 ◦ wide rings at ∼50 ◦ from the magnetic poles. Leahy (1991) derived a large off set angle of ∼63 ◦ between two polar regions, where one was a very broad cap going from the center to an outer angle of ∼62 ◦ while the other was a similarly broad cap of ∼66 ◦ with an inner hole of ∼5◦.Cemeljic and Bulik (1998) discussed how an accretion column passing through the line of sight could qualitatively explain the sharp features seen in the pulse profiles observed by 

BATSE during a giant outburst. Caballero et al. (2011) applied the pulse profile decomposition approach of Kraus et al. (1995) to RXTE /HEXTE pulse profiles. They found a physically acceptable decomposition of the pulse profiles with one magnetic pole at ∼50 ◦ and the other at ∼130 ◦ from one pole of the rotation axis. The two poles are o ff set by ∼25 ◦ from being antipodal. Their model included a hollow column emitting isotropically black body radiation, and a thermal halo around the column on the neutron star surface. Silva et al. (2023) used the code developed by Falkner (2018) to compare with the observed data of an observation during a deep “quiescent” state (see Sect. 5.3.2) observed with NuSTAR .Using cone trunk columns with a fixed height of 1 km, they derived values for the mass and the radius of the neutron star based on two o ff set emission regions with wide semi-aperture angles (∼48 ◦ and ∼72 ◦). The two assumed regions were at latitudes θ1 = 50 ◦.4+2.3 

> −2.0

and θ2 = 321 ◦.1+1.7

> −1.7

with the second pole shifted by ∼ 1◦ in longitude. Hu et al. (2023) analyzed pulse profiles taken by Insight-HXMT during a giant outburst in 2020, based again on the approach of Kraus et al. (1995). They found that the observed pulse profile shape could be described in terms of a combination of two symmetric single-pole contributions for a wide range of energies and luminosities for a fixed pulsar geometry with one pole o ff set by ∼12 ◦ from the antipodal position. Assuming this geometry, they found evidence for a luminosity-dependent transition between pencil and fan beam intrinsic emission patterns at higher energies, above the cyclotron line energy, but a more complex beam pattern combining fan and pencil beam at all luminosities. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 79 ◦.3 ± 1◦.1 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 35 ◦–39 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 

1A 1118 −61 

This slowly pulsating X-ray source was detected with Ariel V by Ives et al. (1975), reporting regular variability at 405 .3 ± 0.6 s, and noted as a candidate for a slowly rotating neutron star by Fabian (1975). Wang and Welter (1981) fitted the rough single pulse shape from Ives et al. (1975) with a pencil beam emission pattern and a polar cap with half-width ≤20 ◦. Leahy (1990) described 97 the same profile by filled polar caps with a half-angle of ∼3◦. Leahy (1991) proposed a quite similar solution with one filled polar cap of half-angle of ∼2◦ and a small ring covering ∼2◦

to ∼3◦ off the opposite pole o ff set, by ∼6◦ from being antipodal. Revisiting these data but including light bending e ff ects, Leahy and Li (1995) derived two narrow polar rings (0 ◦.5 and 0◦.1 wide), both at ∼3◦ from the magnetic poles and a 24 ◦ off set from antipodal. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 45 ◦.4 ± 2◦.1 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 14 ◦.6 ± 1◦.2 for the inclination of the orbit. 

Vela X-1 

Among the earliest known X-ray sources, the relatively slow pulsations ( ∼283 s) were first reported by Rappaport and McClintock (1975). The orbital period of this eclipsing system with the B0.5Ib supergiant HDE 77581 is ∼8.964 d (van Kerkwijk et al. 1995). Mitrofanov and Tsygan (1978) described the low-energy pulse profile (McClintock et al. 1976) as similar to that of 1A 0535 +262 and concluded that one of the columns should be significantly curved. Similar to 1A 0535 +262 (see above) Kanno (1980) explained the energy-dependence of the pulse profile through the e ff ect of scattering in an optically thick, highly magnetized atmosphere. Wang and Welter (1981) explained the hard X-ray pulse profile reported by Staubert et al. (1980) by a non-axisymmetric fan beam. The solution of Leahy (1990) proposed filled polar caps with an opening angle of ∼32 ◦.In contrast, Leahy (1991) modeled the pulse profile with two narrow rings of ∼0◦.5 extent, both ∼37 ◦ from the magnetic poles, o ff set from being antipodal by ∼24 ◦. Leahy and Li (1995) found a quite di ff erent solution for the same data. Their model, which included light bending, had one emitting ring covering ∼25 ◦.0 ± 1◦.5 from its pole and the other covering ∼25 ◦ ± 10 ◦,with the magnetic poles o ff set by ∼10 ◦ from antipodal. Based on data from Nagase (1989), Sturner and Dermer (1994) proposed a description with the observer’s line of sight at 30 ◦ and the B-field axis at 90 ◦ from the rotation axis with a scattering atmosphere at 1 .7 RNS . The multi-peaked profile at lower energies would be caused by the scattering layer eclipsing the polar caps only for a fraction of the rotation period. The match between model and data improved assuming that one cap produced only 80% of the flux from the other. Bulik et al. (1995) derived a configuration with one rather narrow cap, with an angular diameter of ∼4◦, o ff set from one pole of the rotation axis by ∼40 ◦ ± 10 ◦ plus a broader cap (∼17 ◦ diameter) at ∼170 ◦ ± 10 ◦ off this pole. The broader cap would be ∼3.5–4 times brighter than the narrow cap. The angle between the rotation axis and the observer’s line of sight was inferred to be 66 ◦ ± 10 ◦. The model included also an estimate of the neutron star mass, (1 .34 ± 0.16) M⊙ for the “full fit” solution, which is on the low side of mass estimates for the neutron star in this system (see compilation in Kretschmar et al. 2021). Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 88 ◦.8 ± 3◦.0 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 83 ◦.6 ± 3◦.1 for the inclination of the orbit. IXPE reveals low average polarization of 2 .3 ± 0.4% (Forsblom et al. 2023, 2025), which is energy dependent. These very complex polarization properties have been modeled as superposition of two components. The polarization below 3 keV is very complex and di ffi cult to constrain. At energies above 3 keV the pulse profile is much less complex and systematic, 98 sinusoidal variations in the degree of polarization become apparent. Some of the variation on position angle at these energies can be described with the rotating vector model, with an estimated magnetic obliquity of 13 ◦. However, more complex explanations utilizing additional components are also possible (Wu et al. 2026). 

SMC X-1 

Lucke et al. (1975) first reported X-ray pulsations from SMC X-1. The pulse period is ∼0.7 s and the system is in a ∼3.9 d orbit. A first pulse profile was published in Primini et al. (1977). In their modeling of that profile, Wang and Welter (1981) assumed a “pencil beam” (cosine) flux distribution from two similar broad ( ∼55 ◦) polar caps and relative large angles of ∼56 ◦

and ∼79 ◦ for the angles between line of sight and rotation or magnetic axis and rotation axis. They noted that for such an intrinsically bright source they would have rather assumed an extended column with a fan-beam pattern. Based on the high-energy pulse profile shown by White et al. (1983), Leahy (1990) derived as emission pattern in his symmetrical model a relatively broad polar ring with inner and outer angles of ∼35 ◦ and ∼47 ◦. For the same data, but allowing for two di ff erent polar regions, Leahy (1991) found as a preferred solution one broad and one narrow polar ring with inner and outer angles of ∼21 ◦ and ∼40 ◦ for one side and ∼37 ◦ and ∼40 ◦ for the other. The two poles are o ff set from opposition by ∼17 ◦.Using the Polestar model, Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 77 ◦.3 ± 1◦.4 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 67 ◦.8 ± 4◦.2 for the inclination of the orbit. The analysis of three IXPE observations by Forsblom et al. (2024) reveals polarization degrees around 3%. A phase resolved analysis finds polarization hovering around ∼2% with the exception of 2 /7 phase bins where it is increased to ∼7%. A slight modulation of PA is seen (amplitude about 10 ◦ with large error bars). Applying the rotating vector model, Forsblom et al. (2024) find an average magnetic inclination of 13 +7 

> −7

deg and an inclination of 91 +41  

> −42

deg. Separate analysis of the three observations indicates possible systematic variation of the polarization angle which Forsblom et al. interpret as a possible evolution due to the super-orbital phase, either due to precession of the neutron star or the accretion disk. 

X Per 

X-ray pulsations of this Be X-ray binary ( Porb ∼ 250 d) were first reported with a period of 

∼835 s by White et al. (1976b) based on observations by Ariel V and Copernicus , and soon after from SAS 3 observations by Rappaport and Joss (1977) Wang and Welter (1981) used the profile published by Rappaport and Joss (1977). Due to the relatively low luminosity they first tried to model the profile with a pencil beam leading to a broad polar cap with a half-angle of 45 ◦, but noted that a somewhat better description could be obtained with a fan-beam with a half-angle of 30 ◦.Leahy (1990) fitted the profile from White et al. (1983) with a filled polar cap of 8 ◦ half-angle. Leahy (1991) had the two magnetic poles almost antipodal (o ff set <6◦), one covered by filled polar cap with a half-angle of 4 ◦ and the other a moderately broad ring at 5 ◦.7 ± 5◦.7 from the pole. 99 Robba et al. (1996) included in their discussion of a Ginga observation a simple model of fan emission from a hollow accretion column ranging to several neutron star radii, not mentioning the model results by Leahy (1991). Analysis of the IXPE data shows that the non-detection of polarization in the phase averaged data is due to large changes in the degree of polarization and polarization angle with pulse phase (Mushtukov et al. 2023). Simple RVM modeling yields an inclination of 162 ◦ ± 12 ◦, consistent with the orbital inclination (around 26 ◦, note that the RVM inclination is either i or 180 ◦ − i) and a magnetic obliquity of being close to 90 ◦, with a lower limit of 78 ◦

at 68% confidence. 

GX 301 −2

Pulsations of around 680 s were detected in Ariel V observations (White et al. 1976a, as 3U 1223 −62) and also by SAS 3 (Rappaport and Joss 1977). The orbital period is 41.5 d. Wang and Welter (1981) explained the profile reported by Rappaport and Joss with wide pencil beam emission with a half-angle of 35 ◦. The inclination between rotation and magnetic dipole axis appeared to be between 50 ◦ and 80 ◦.Leahy (1990) modeled the profile obtained from Tenma observations 7 with a very broad filled polar cap (half-angle 40 ◦). Leahy (1991) labeled the source as 4U 1223 −62 and the data source is cited to be White et al. (1983), who had their profile based on OSO-8 . In this case, the preferred solution of the emission model was one broad ring ranging from ∼13 ◦ to ∼33 ◦

and a narrow, just 2 ◦ wide, ring at ∼27 ◦ from the respective magnetic poles, which are o ff set by ∼21 ◦ from being antipodal. Borkus et al. (1998b) fitted a hard X-ray pulse profile obtained with the HEXE instrument on-board the MIR-Kvant module, building on Pechenick et al. (1983) and Ri ff ert and M´ esz´ aros (1988). They found beamed emission from two identical, thin annular rings with opening angles of 6 ◦ and width 0 ◦.6, with one pole o ff set by ∼10 ◦ from being diametrically opposite of the other. The angle between magnetic axis and spin axis was estimated to be 50 ◦, with an angle of 74 ◦.5 between the observer’s direction and the spin axis. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 78 ◦.6 ± 1◦.1 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 55 ◦–75 ◦ for the orbital inclination. Suleimanov et al. (2023) find no significant polarization in the average IXPE spectrum, but phase dependent polarization is present in phase resolved analysis, typically below a few per cent. The polarization angle varies in a sawtooth-like manner which can be explained with the rotating vector model, yielding an inclination of 129 ◦ ± 16 ◦ and a magnetic obliquity of 47 +13  

> −14

deg (Suleimanov et al. 2023). 

4U 1626 −67 

X-ray pulsations at 7.68 s from this source were detected by Rappaport et al. (1977). The neutron star accretes from a 0 .04 M⊙ donor and is in a 42 minute orbit (Chakrabarty 1998). Wang and Welter (1981) described the emission of this pulsar with a fan beam pattern and a moderate inclination to the observer, but noted that the variability of the pulse shape left large uncertainties.  

> 7Presumably – we could not find the profile reference cited in the paper (Leahy, Matsuoka, Kawai & Makino MNRAS in press ).

100 Kii et al. (1986a,b) studied energy resolved pulse profiles observed with Tenma which showed a drastic shape change at ∼15 keV. They found that this could not be explained by a simple geometrical model of pencil or fan beam emission but required to take into account the radiative transfer in a strongly magnetized plasma. They modeled matching pulse profiles assuming emission from thin cylinders (radius 10 m) at the magnetic poles and angles of 40 ◦

and 50 ◦ for the angles between rotation and magnetic axis and rotation axis and line of sight, respectively. The B-field strength was assumed to be 8 × 10 12 G. Leahy (1990) modeled the emission by wide polar rings at 55 ± 5◦ from the poles. Leahy (1991) provided two solutions which di ff er strongly from the 1990 result. Both solutions required one narrow cap with an opening angle of 4 ◦ or 6 ◦ and one ring at 14 ◦ ± 3◦ off set from the direct opposite position by ∼6◦. A very similar solution was also found by Leahy and Li (1995). Sturner and Dermer (1994) described the pulse profile with a scattering atmosphere at 2.175 RNS , with the observer’s line of sight at 27 ◦ and the B-field axis at 45 ◦ from the rotation axis. Iwakiri et al. (2019) applied the modeling code of Falkner (2018), assuming a “canonical” neutron star ( MNS = 1.4M⊙, RNS = 10 km), two cylindrical columns of identical heights and radii, placed at independent azimuthal ( Φ) and polar angles ( Θ) relative to the rotation axis. The simplified emission model consisted of mixture of Gaussian-like fan- and pencil-beam emission components in the frame of rest of the neutron star’s surface and varying emission parameters as function of energy. The columns have a width of 590 ± 50 m and height of 252 +12  

> −14

m and are placed at ( Φ1, Θ1) = (76 ◦.6 ± 0◦.3, 12 ◦.5+0.07 

> −0.11

) and ( Φ2, Θ2) = (290 ◦ ± 2◦, 158 ◦.1 ± 0◦.2), respectively. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 9 ◦.6 ± 2◦.2 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 11 ◦–36 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. Marshall et al. (2022) find an average degree of polarization of around 3% with little variation over the pulse. Modeling using the rotating vector model yields an inclination of 44 34 22 deg and a magnetic obliquity of 71 ◦.912 ◦.5

4U 1538 −52 

Pulsations at 528 .29 ± 0.10 s were detected independently by Ariel V and OSO-8 during the discovery of this source (Davison 1977; Davison et al. 1977; Becker et al. 1977), but this HMXB, shows strong pulse period variability (Hemphill et al. 2014). The neutron star is in a 3.73 d orbit with the O-star QV Nor, the X-ray source also exhibits a 14.9 d super-orbital period, consistent exactly 4 × Porb (Corbet et al. 2021; Cohen et al. 2026). Similar to GX 301 −2, Wang and Welter (1981) explained the pulse profile with pencil beam emission from a wide polar cap with opening angle ∼35 ◦. The inclination between rotation and magnetic dipole axis appeared to between 50 ◦ and 80 ◦.Clark et al. (1990) compared their Ginga observations of the pulse profile and spectra with models based on Nagel (1981b) and M´ esz´ aros and Nagel (1985a). Finding a cylindrical column to be unable to explain the data, they fitted the radiative transfer results of (Nagel 1981b) for a slab model and derived an angle of 65 ◦ between the rotation angle and the line of sight and one of 45 ◦ between the rotation axis and the magnetic dipole axis. Cominsky and Moraes (1991) used a slightly simplified version of the model of Parmar et al. (1989) for EXO 2030 +375 (see below, p. 105) to a EXOSAT /MED pulse profile. They 101 derived an angle of 58 ◦.89 ± 0◦.57 for the angle between the rotation axis and the line of sight and angles of 43 ◦.66 ± 0◦.57 and 188 ◦.25 ± 0◦.57 between one pole of the rotation axis and the two magnetic poles. In this model the broader and higher peak in the profile is almost completely comprised of flux from the two fan beams from both poles, whereas a single pencil beam is responsible for the emission during the smaller peak. Sturner and Dermer (1994) proposed a description with a scattering atmosphere at 2 .1 RNS 

and one pole being twice as X-ray bright as the other, where the observer’s line of sight is inclined by 70 ◦ and the B-field axis at 55 ◦ from the rotation axis. In their “full fit” model Bulik et al. (1995) derived a configuration with two rather narrow polar caps with angular diameters of ∼4◦ and ∼2◦, respectively. The narrower cap is brighter by a factor ∼2.7. The former cap would be at 54 ◦ ± 14 ◦, the latter at 112 ◦ ± 10 ◦ from one pole of the rotation axis. The angle to the line of sight to the observer was inferred as 67 ◦ ± 10 ◦,very similar to the solution for Vela X-1, listed above. Using the Polestar model, Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 76 ◦.5 ± 0◦.9 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 72 ◦.6 ± 4◦.2 for the inclination of the orbit. Maniadakis et al. (2025) describe the emission using the FINRAD code (Sokolova-Lapa et al. 2021, 2023) combined with the ray tracing code of Falkner (2018) for the projection onto the plane of a distant observer. Their adopted model had an inclination between rotation axis and line of sight of i = 67 ◦ with two emitting spots of ∼300 m radius at angles of 80 ◦ and 105 ◦

to the rotation axis and 168 ◦ apart in longitude. Loktev et al. (2025) found the average IXPE polarization to be at 3 .0 ± 1.1%, with some evidence for energy dependent polarization, with the polarization angle changing by about 70 ◦

at 3.5 keV. The authors speculate this to be due to vacuum resonance e ff ects (similar to claims in Vela X-1), but soft excess e ff ects cannot be excluded. In pulse phase resolved spectroscopy, the polarization degree is around 5% in most phase bins, with an increase to about 12% in one phase bin during the main peak, and large scatter in the polarization angle. Due to the strong energy dependency of the polarization parameters the behavior cannot be explained with the rotating vector model. 

GX 304 −1

Observations by SAS-3 revealed regular pulsations at ∼272 s (McClintock et al. 1977) for this Be-X-ray binary in a 132.19 d orbit (Sugizaki et al. 2015). Leahy (1990, labeling the source 4U 1258 −62) reported a solution with two broad polar rings at ∼19 ◦ ±5◦ from the magnetic poles. Leahy (1991) provided two possible, quite di ff erent, solutions for this source, with no clear preference from the fit quality. One solution had two narrow polar rings, both ∼1◦ wide, at ∼28 ◦ and ∼24 ◦ from their respective poles, which were off set by ∼29 ◦ from antipodal. The other had one almost filled polar cap out to ∼30 ◦ from one pole and a ∼5◦ wide ring at ∼28 ◦ from the other, o ff set by ∼25 ◦ from the antipodal position. The model of Leahy and Li (1995) with light bending had the poles o ff set by ∼12 ◦ from being antipodal, with one emission ring at 27 ◦.3 ± 0◦.6 and the other at 23 ◦.8 ± 1◦.7. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 22 ◦.2 ± 1◦.7 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 60 ◦–80 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 102 4U 0115 +63 

The source was noted as probably showing a transient outburst during Uhuru observations of the Galactic Plane (Forman et al. 1976). Cominsky et al. (1978) first reported pulsations at 

∼3.6 s based on SAS 3 observations in this Be X-ray binary ( Porb ∼ 24 .3 d). Leahy (1990) derived narrow, ∼3◦ wide rings at ∼44 ◦ from the poles. The preferred solution in Leahy (1991) had two even narrower rings of ∼2◦ width, at ∼35 ◦ and ∼33 ◦ from the two poles, o ff set by ∼34 ◦ from being antipodal. Including light bending, Leahy and Li (1995) derived a very similar solution narrow rings again, but now with almost perfectly antipodal poles o ff set by just 0 ◦.4. Sasaki et al. (2012) decomposed pulse profiles observed with RXTE during one outburst following the approach of Kraus et al. (1995). The decomposition led to two polar regions off set by 65 +14  

> −26

degrees from antipodal. As in many other approaches, the main uncertainty is due to the little known or unknown inclination between the line of sight and the rotation axis of the neutron star. Near the peak of the outburst, the beam patterns could be interpreted in terms of an accretion stream with a column and a halo at the bottom, while towards the end of the outburst at luminosities of about one tenth of the peak value a pencil beam emanating from a hot spot was su ffi cient, possibly modulated by scattering in the accretion stream. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 52 ◦.9 ± 2◦.1 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 40 ◦–60 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 

OAO 1657 −415 

White and Pravdo (1979) reported the detection of ∼38 s pulsations from this eclipsing super-giant HMXB in HEAO 1 pointed observations. The orbital period is ∼10.4 d (Chakrabarty et al. 1993). Wang and Welter (1981) explained the clearly asymmetric shape of the pulses with a fan beam pattern where the width in the direction of rotation was di ff erent from the trailing side. Leahy (1990, labeling the source as OAO 1653 −40) modeled the profile with antipodal, moderately broad, rings at ∼39 ◦ ± 7◦ from the magnetic poles. In contrast, the solution given by Leahy (1991) had two very narrow rings, 0 ◦.5 wide at ∼43 ◦ off their respective poles which were o ff set by 86 .5◦ from being antipodal. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 25 ◦.9 ± 1◦.1 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 60 ◦–90 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 

1E 1145.1 −6141 

This source is one of the two “twin pulsars” pulsating at similar frequencies and located within 

<20 ′ from each other (White et al. 1978, 1980). This supergiant HMXB has a pulse period of 

∼296.7 s and an orbital period of 14.4 d (Ray and Chakrabarty 2002b). Wang and Welter (1981) proposed a non-axisymmetric flux distribution produced uniformly over polar caps with an opening angle of ∼5◦. The radiation was assumed to escape freely on the leading side of the magnetic pole but scatter sideways on the trailing side where the plasma density was presumed to be higher. The inclination between rotation and magnetic dipole axis appeared to between 50 ◦ and 80 ◦.103 2S 1145 −619 

The other “twin pulsar” (see above) found by (White et al. 1980), a Be-star X-ray binary with the optical companion V801 Cen with a pulse period of 292.4 s and an orbital period of 186.7 d (Alfonso-Garz´ on et al. 2017, and references therein). The source is sometimes also called H1145 −619. Wang and Welter (1981) found this source to be similar to OAO 1657 −415 (see above), with an asymmetric pulse shape explained by a fan beam pattern with di ff erent widths in the direction of rotation vs the trailing side. They also concluded that this pulsar was in the group with an inclination <50 ◦ between rotation and magnetic dipole axis. Leahy (1990) described the profiles with emission by two rings at 42 ±2◦ from the antipodal magnetic poles. Leahy (1991), calling the source 4U 1145 −61, provided two equally rated solutions. The first had two moderately narrow rings (5 ◦.7 and 3 ◦.4 wide) at 32 ◦.7 and 32 ◦.7 from their poles, respectively with an o ff set of 18 ◦.9. The second had the latter ring unchanged, but the first replaced by a hollow polar cap opening from 2 ◦.9 to 39 ◦.5, with the o ff set reduced to 6 ◦.3. 

4U 1907 +09 

This accreting pulsar was among the sources found in early X-ray scans by Uhuru (Giacconi et al. 1971b, , as X1) and Ariel V (Seward et al. 1976). Pulsations at ∼437 .5 s were first detected in a 1983 Tenma observation (Makishima et al. 1984). The neutron star is in an eccentric orbit ( e = 0.28, Porb = 8.38 d) around its O-star donor. Sturner and Dermer (1994) described the pulse profile with the observer’s line of sight at 30 ◦ and the B-field axis at 45 ◦ from the rotation axis with a scattering atmosphere at 1.8 or 1.2 RNS for the pulses observed at 3–6 keV or 10–30 keV, respectively (Makishima et al. 1984). In the two IXPE observations, 4U 1907 +09 was strongly variable with flaring and dipping events, although these do not a ff ect the polarization measurement Zhou et al. (2025). Weak polarization is detected in the average spectrum (below 6%). The polarization angle depends on energy, albeit with a significance of only 1 .7σ, but this precludes application of the RVM. 

V 0332 +53 

This BeXRB was first observed by Vela 5B in 1973, but published much later (Terrell and Priedhorsky 1984). Stella et al. (1985) detected stable 4.4 s pulsations and the ∼34.3 d orbit during EXOSAT observations of three outbursts between November 1983 and January 1984. Sasaki et al. (2012) applied the pulse-profile decomposition method of Kraus et al. (1995) to pulse profiles observed with RXTE during a bright outburst between December 2004 and January 2005. The decomposition led to two polar regions o ff set by 12 +24  

> −1

deg from antipodal. At the start and end of the outburst emission could be modeled by a pencil beam pattern, while during the brighter phases fan beam emission from a – possibly hollow – column and a halo of scattered photons was found. Analyzing the variability of the RXTE pulse profiles from the same outburst, Mushtukov et al. (2024) published evidence for the eclipsing of the accretion column by the accretion stream as the main reason for the deep troughs observed during a narrow section of the pulse cycle. 104 EXO 2030 +375 

This transient X-ray pulsar ( P ∼ 42 s) was detected by EXOSAT during one of its bright outbursts (Parmar et al. 1985). It is in a 46.02 d orbit. Parmar et al. (1989) modeled the changing pulse profiles of this source during an outburst using a simple geometric model where axially symmetric fan and pencil-beams of emission are emitted from two magnetic poles, motivated by Wang and Welter (1981). The profile’s marked asymmetry was modeled by o ff setting the magnetic dipole axis from the rotation axis of the neutron star. Gravitational light bending was approximated by including a fixed deflection angle independent of height. Results were mainly modeled assuming polar cap positions at (latitude, longitude) of (20 ◦, 20 ◦) and ( −20 ◦, 125 ◦) corresponding to a subtended angle of 

∼110 ◦. Variations of the pulse profiles with luminosity were explained by varying ratios both for the relative strength of the overall emissions from the poles as for the relative strength of fan vs pencil beam emission with high luminosity states being dominated by fan beam emission. Leahy and Li (1995) attempted to trace changes with luminosity in the pulse profiles obtained with EXOSAT . They found that the changes in the observed profiles as function of luminosity could not be explained using their pencil beam model, since there we no satisfactory solution for the deep notches and sharp peaks seen in the profiles, and that a more complex fan beam model would probably be required. Sasaki et al. (2010) undertook a detailed study using the decomposition approach of Kraus et al. (1995) of 26 pulse profiles observed with RXTE and INTEGRAL during a giant outburst in 2006. They discussed di ff erent possible geometries and the changes of the beam pattern with energy. Assuming an observing angle of 50 ◦ between line of sight and the rotation axis, their preferred solution had one magnetic pole at 39 ◦ and the other at 141 ◦ from the reference rotation axis pole and an o ff set of 40 ◦ from being antipodal for the magnetic poles. They noted that their results would be consistent with those of Parmar et al. (1989) when assuming an observing angle of 25 ◦. Based on the beam pattern modeling by Kraus et al. (2003) for column and halo emission, for one example profile taken near the maximum of the outburst Sasaki et al. proposed a solution where about half of the pulse phase is dominated by halo emission from the first pole, then an interval of about 0.2 in phase seeing emission from the second pole plus a similarly extended contribution from the accretion column of the first pole. Thalhammer et al. (2024) discussed pulse profiles from concurrent observations with NuS-TAR and NICER of a giant outburst in 2021 applying the code of Falkner (2018) to model profiles that would match the observed ones, providing results for high and low luminosity. They found that they could explain the changes in the observed profiles as function of lumi-nosity could be explained using a stable geometric configuration with emission from the wall and top of two accretion columns but the emission profiles of these components changing as function of luminosity. The two columns would also have varying relative contributions to the overall flux. In the modeling both columns were fixed to radius of 250 m and a height of 300 m, in line with theoretical estimates. The observing angle was fixed to 130 ◦ based on the results of IXPE observations (Malacaria et al. 2023b). Similar to Parmar et al. (1989) and Sasaki et al. (2010) the asymmetric profiles were explained by non-antipodal magnetic poles, the angle subtended between these poles was 119 ◦ between the values given in the previous studies. One magnetic pole was found at 75 ◦.6 ± 0◦.6 and the other at 116 +26  

> −11

deg from the reference pole on the rotation axis. 105 Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 57 ◦.0 ± 1◦.0 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 48 ◦–57 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. In IXPE observations, Malacaria et al. (2023b) find an average 2–8 keV degree of polariza-tion of ∼3%. The degree of polarization shows complex pulse phase dependency, the position angle has a “zig-zag” type behavior with an oscillation amplitude of around ±100 ◦. RVM modeling by Malacaria et al. (2023b) shows a pulsar inclination of 129 +9 

> −7

deg with a magnetic obliquity of 59 +5 

> −6

deg. 

GS 1843 −024 

This source was identified as an accreting BeXRB ( Pspin = 94 .8 ± 0.1 s) with Ginga (Makino and GINGA Team 1988a). Leahy (1991) gave two equally ranked solutions. The first had two rings at ∼63 ◦ ± 1◦ and ∼59 ◦ ± 4◦ from the magnetic poles, who were o ff set by ∼38 ◦ from being antipodal. The second solution had the rings at comparable distances from the poles as in the first but more similar in width ( ∼63 ◦ ± 5◦ and ∼58 ◦ ± 6◦) and almost antipodal poles with a very small o ff set of 1 ◦.

IGR J17252 −3616 (GS 1722 −36) 

This accreting pulsar was first noted as X-ray source in a survey of the Galactic Plane with 

EXOSAT (Warwick et al. 1988). It was identified as a slowly rotating pulsar ( Pspin = 413 .9 s) through a Ginga observation (Tawara et al. 1989). In follow up observations in 2004 with 

INTEGRAL (Walter et al. 2004) and XMM-Newton , the source was found at a position significantly di ff erent from the EXOSAT position, but by its properties are consistent with those of the EXOSAT source (Zurita Heras et al. 2006), and the orbit was determined to 

Porb = 9.72 d. Leahy (1991) gave two, quite di ff erent, equally ranked solutions. The first had a very broad hollow polar cap covering from ∼4◦ to ∼57 ◦ around one pole and a narrow ring at ∼50 ◦ ± 2◦

around the others with the two poles ∼77 ◦ off set from being antipodal. In the second solution two equally shaped hollow polar caps ranged from ∼6◦ to ∼44 ◦ with an o ff set angle of ∼43 ◦.The solution provided by Leahy and Li (1995) came closer to the first of the previously given. It included a very broad hollow polar cap covering from ∼6◦ to ∼42 ◦ around one magnetic pole and <1◦ wide ring at ∼39 ◦ off the other pole which was o ff set by ∼39 ◦ from being antipodal. 

GS 1843 +00 

This source, sometimes also called GS 1843 +009, was identified as an accreting X-ray pulsar (Pspin = 29 .508 ± 0.002 s) by Ginga (Makino and GINGA Team 1988c; Koyama et al. 1990). The Be companion was found by Israel et al. (2001), the orbital period appears to be around 50 d (Seifina 2007). The strongly preferred solution of Leahy (1991, calling the source GS 1840 +00) had one 

∼7 deg wide ring at ∼62 ◦ and another ∼8 deg wide ring at ∼56 ◦ from their respective magnetic poles which were o ff set by ∼18 ◦ from antipodal. As for the case of Cen X-3 (see above, p. 92), Ri ff ert et al. (1993) found important qualitative di ff erences with the results of Leahy (1991). Their example solution had two polar caps with an opening angle of 30 ◦ each, a near orthogonal orientation of the rotation axis with 106 respect to the observer, and large values for the angle between the rotation and the magnetic axis as well as for the o ff set of the second cap from the antipodal position. 

2S 0114 +650 

The nature of this source and identification as a very slow X-ray pulsar with a pulse period of 

∼2.7 h ( ∼9400 s) was debated for a long time (Finley et al. 1992; Hall et al. 2000). The donor is the B1 supergiant V662 Cas. With Porb ∼ 11 .6 d the neutron star is deeply embedded in its stellar wind (Sanjurjo-Ferr´ ın et al. 2025b, and references therein). A 30.7 d super-orbital period was initially discovered from RXTE ASM observations by Farrell et al. (2006). Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 46 ◦.1 ± 1◦.3 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 45 ◦–50 ◦ for the orbital inclination. 

KS 1947 +300 

This Be X-ray binary was first detected as a transient source by the TTM instrument onboard the Mir /Kvant module (Skinner 1989; Borozdin et al. 1990). Chakrabarty et al. (1995) detected pulsations at 18.7 s in CGRO /BATSE observations of the Cygnus region, labeling the source GRO J1948 +32. The orbtial period is 40.415 d (Galloway et al. 2004). Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 12 ◦.1 ± 0◦.8 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 28 ◦–57 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 

GRO J1008 −57 

This source was detected as a new hard X-ray pulsar ( Pspin ∼ 93 .5 s with small changes between detections) by CGRO /BATSE (Wilson et al. 1994). The orbital period of this BeXRB is 248.9 d (K¨ uhnel et al. 2013, and references therein). Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 47 ◦.1 ± 2◦.1 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 26 ◦–46 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 

SXP 348 

Detected in 1998 as a X-ray pulsar ( Pspin = 345 .2 ± 0.1 s) during a BeppoSAX obser-vation of a region in the Small Magellanic Cloud (SMC) (Israel et al. 1998) and labeled 1SAX J0103.2 −7209, the source is at a position consistent with the Einstein source 2E 0101.5 −7225 and the ROSAT source RX J0103.2 −7209 (Israel et al. 2000). OGLE data of the counterpart, the Be-star [MA93] 1367, shows weak indications of a periodicity of ∼94 d (Schmidtke and Cowley 2006, but see Rajoelimanana et al. 2011). As it is with in the field of view of a Chandra calibration target, the source has regularly observed. SXP 348 has a complex evolution, including a a phase during 2002 where the source remained bright but no pulsations could be detected in XMM-Newton or Chandra 

observations (Cappallo et al. 2019). The general pulse profile shape changed from indications of a double-peak structure before 2002 to a quite sinusoidal single-peak pattern afterwards. Cappallo et al. (2019) presented the results of modeling 71 unique pulse profiles of SXB 348 with Polestar , taken from the “observational library” of data products for X-ray pulsars in the SMC (Yang et al. 2017). They presented results both for a four parameter model, representing simplified pencil beam emission from opposing poles and for a six parameter model allowing 107 for a fan beam component. Individual profile fits scattered strongly for all model parameters, including the inclination (observing angle) i between line of sight and rotation axis and the angle θ between the magnetic axis and the rotation axis. Combining statistical arguments with assumptions like no jump in inclination before and after 2002, they suggested as most likely overall geometry i ∼ 65 ◦ ± 2◦.5 and θ ∼ 20 ◦ ± 7◦.

XTE J1946 +274 

Detected as a transient source in 1998 by RXTE /ASM and identified as a pulsar ( Pspin =

15 .83 ± 0.02 s) in RXTE /ASM follow-up observations (Smith et al. 1998), this neutron star BeXRB is in a 172 d orbit. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 80 ◦.1 ± 1◦.3 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of >46 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 

SMC X-2 

This source was already detected in the 1970s with SAS 3 (Li et al. 1977), but pulsations were found only much later with RXTE by Corbet and Marshall (2000) at 2 .374 ± 0.007 s. The orbital period is about 18.4 d (La Palombara et al. 2016). Roy et al. (2022) applied the 

Polestar model to four pulse profiles obtained with XMM-Newton and NuSTAR during a giant outburst in 2015. They found the most likely inclination of the spin axis of the pulsar to be i = 87 ◦ ± 4◦, while for the magnetic inclination between rotation and magnetic axis they suggested a most probable value ≤50 ◦.

4U 1909 +07 

4U 1909 +07 was discovered as a relatively faint X-ray and highly absorbed source in various X-ray surveys (Wen et al. 2000). The OB donor star and the neutron star are in a 4.4 d orbit. Corbet and Krimm (2013) found a 15.18 d super-orbital period from Swift BAT observations. Based on RXTE /PCA observations, Levine et al. (2004, calling the source X1907 +075) reported pulsations at ∼605 s. Laycock et al. IGR J16393 −4643 

This source was first discovered during a survey of the Galactic plane by ASCA and named AX J1639.0 −4642 (Sugizaki et al. 2001). Bodaghee et al. (2006) detected pulsations at 912 .0 ±

0.1 s in INTEGRAL /ISGRI and XMM-Newton /EPIC light curves. It is an eclipsing wind fed system in a 4.2 d orbit (Corbet and Krimm 2013; Coley et al. 2015). A candidate 14.98 d super-orbital period from Swift BAT observations (Corbet and Krimm 2013; Coley et al. 2019; Corbet et al. 2021) remains to be confirmed. Laycock et al. (2025) derived an inclination of 12 ◦.7 ± 1◦.9 for the pulsar spin axis relative to the line of sight, compared to an estimate of 39 ◦–77 ◦ for the inclination of the orbit. 108 RX J0440.9 +4431 /LS V +44 17 

The source was identified as a possible X-ray binary by Motch et al. (1997)and confirmed an BeXRB pulsar with a 206 s period by Reig and Roche (1999b). The orbital period has been estimated to 150 d (Ferrigno et al. 2013). The analysis of two IXPE observations with a single RVM yields very di ff erent parameters. The inclination varies from 50 ◦ to 70 ◦ in one observation, and is around 100 ◦ in another observation, while the pulsar position angle switches by about 90 ◦ and the magnetic obliquity changes from ∼30 ◦ to ∼54 ◦ (Doroshenko et al. 2023). A possible explanation of the change in position angle could be a switch of the polarization mode between the extraordinary to the ordinary mode, but this does not explain the changes in inclination and magnetic obliquity (Doroshenko et al. 2023). As an alternative, Doroshenko et al. (2023) propose a two component model with a polarized and an unpolarized component. Such a model yields an inclination of 108 ◦ ± 2◦ and a magnetic obliquity of 48 ◦ ± 1◦. Zhao et al. (2025) confirm these results and study how the constant component evolves with time.

## Appendix D Comparison with other source types 

Beyond the “classical”, that is, sub-Eddington accretion, high B-field, accreting X-ray pulsars discussed in this review (the focus class), here we provide brief sketches of the behavior of these other classes, highlighting similarities and di ff erences together with pointers to salient references. We attempt to answer three questions: (1) what are the understood characteristics of the class in question? (2) Why is the class out of scope for this review? (3) In what ways does the class nevertheless overlap or perhaps constrain the theory presented in the review, even when it does not have much in common with it observationally? 

D.1 Supergiant Fast X-ray Transients 

Supergiant Fast X-ray Transients (SFXTs) are high-mass binaries detected during flares of short duration, typically lasting 100–10 000 s and reaching a dynamic X-ray flux range of 

∼100, occasionally reaching luminosities exceeding 10 38 erg s −1 (Sidoli 2013; Romano et al. 2013; Negueruela 2019). The flares are fast and numerous: a Swift /BAT catalog covering 100 months finds 1117 flares from 11 SFXT sources, for five of these pulsations have been reported, but di ff erent studies do not always agree (Romano et al. 2023, and references therein). Overall, observed pulsations are scarce. SFXTs appear to occupy a preferred region in the plane defined by orbital period and eccentricity, models of their behavior have been developed using the idea of clumped accretion flow onto a neutron star (see, for example, Karino 2010, and references therein). Accretion may be irregular due to magnetic or centrifugal gating (see Sect. 3.1). The study of SFXTs as borderline cases and may provide insights into the physical conditions under which accretion penetrates magnetospheres with resulting formation of pulsations. But the scarcity of pulsation detections, and consequent lack of a clear picture regarding their light curves, precludes further inclusion in our study. 

D.2 Ultra-Luminous X-ray Pulsars 

Ultra-Luminous X-ray sources (ULXs) are o ff -nuclear point-sources in nearby galaxies that persistently exceed luminosities of 10 39 erg s −1, assuming isotropic emission at the measured distance of the host galaxy. They have long been suspected to be accreting intermediate mass ( ∼10 4 M⊙) black holes (Makishima et al. 2000; King et al. 2001), but the discovery of 110 pulsations from a number of bright ULXs clearly identified the compact object as a neutron star (e.g., Bachetti et al. 2014; F¨ urst et al. 2016; Israel et al. 2017a,b). These ultra-luminous X-ray pulsars (ULXPs), have numerous similarities with HMXBs in our own galaxy, except that they are 10–100 times more luminous and can show extreme spin period changes (e.g., Vasilopoulos et al. 2018; Bachetti et al. 2020). The pulse profiles of the known ULXPs are relatively simple and almost sinusoidal, with only one smooth main peak visible. Neither a strong energy nor a strong luminosity dependence has been observed. ULXPs are typically only observed at energies < 30 keV, due to their low fluxes. The pulsed fraction increases with energy, similar to sources in the Milky Way (F¨ urst et al. 2016). The simple shape of the pulse profiles may be due to sinusoidal pulsations being easier to identify in low signal-to-noise data. Alternatively it could be caused by strong reprocessing within the the super-Eddington accretion flow around the neutron star engulfing large parts of the magnetosphere and acting as a low-pass filter (Mushtukov et al. 2017). 

D.3 Accreting Millisecond X-ray Pulsars 

Accreting Millisecond X-ray Pulsars (AMXPs) are mildly magnetized neutron stars accreting from a low mass star or white dwarf companion via Roche lobe overflow while pulsating at pulse periods of only a few milliseconds, detected during outbursts (see Di Salvo and Sanna 2022 for a recent overview). The fast pulse periods with ongoing accretion imply much lower 

B-field strengths (Sect. 3.1, Eq. 5) than in the accreting X-ray pulsars reviewed in this paper – usually around 10 8–10 9 G. Thus they cover a very di ff erent range in the parameter space of accretion physics than the sources we focus on. The observed pulse profiles of AMXPs tend to have a simple, near sinusoidal shape (e.g., Cui et al. 1998; Ford 2000; Kirsch et al. 2004; Poutanen 2006); but observations also find additional harmonics or secondary structures, such as leading or trailing shoulders in some sources (Strohmayer et al. 2003; Sanna et al. 2022; Sharma et al. 2023b; Li et al. 2023). The pulse shape tends to not change significantly with energy, but the fractional amplitude, especially of the fundamental clearly varies with energy (e.g., Ng et al. 2021; Li et al. 2023). The pulse profiles commonly show a shift in the peak emission as function of energy with pulses at energies of a few keV lagging those at energies at tens of keV (Cui et al. 1998; Ford 2000), and AMXPs show various temporal variations, including intermittent double pulses and jumps in pulse phase (e.g., Poutanen et al. 2009; Patruno et al. 2009). A subgroup of AMXPs, the Transitional Millisecond Pulsars, swing between a phase with accretion-powered X-ray pulsations and a phase with rotation-powered radio pulsations. The first such system was discovered by Papitto et al. (2013), a recent overview is provided by Papitto and de Martino (2022). Their X-ray activity is characterized by outbursts as luminous as those of AMXPs. Their pulse profiles are usually broad, roughly sinusoidal and with low pulsed fractions (e.g., De Falco et al. 2017; Illiano et al. 2023). Their lower-luminosity phase is characterized by three states: flaring, active, passive. In the active and flaring states, pulse profiles are similar to those observed during the outburst phase, while they are strongly suppressed in the passive mode. Mildly magnetized neutron stars can also exhibit thermonuclear X-ray bursts due to the unstable burning of matter in the outer neutron star layers following extended periods of accumulating material. Thousands of such bursts have been observed from more than 80 sources (e.g., Galloway et al. 2020). The frequent generalization that accreting pulsars do not 111 exhibit X-ray bursts is valid as a common observation for the classical X-ray pulsars discussed in the main part of this review but not for AMXPs where thermonuclear bursts are observed in many sources. Brown and Bildsten (1998) found that a magnetic field strength of ∼ 10 10 G or higher is required to keep the matter accreted at the poles from spreading. Thus for the higher magnetic field X-ray pulsars on which our review is focusing the local accretion rate is boosted and nuclear burning assumed to be stable, in contrast to the AMXPs. Yet another case is the “Bursting Pulsar”, GRO J1744 −28, which shows clear pulsations and bursts, but the latter are caused by unstable accretion (Finger et al. 1996a; K¨ onig et al. 2020, and references therein), which also leaves this peculiar source o ff point for this review. In some of these bursts, a strong periodic signal is found, which Strohmayer et al. (1996) in their report of the first clearly detected example labeled ‘burst oscillations’. The frequencies of these oscillations were soon connected to the rotation frequencies of the neutron stars, in various cases confirmed by finding persistent or intermittent pulsations from the ongoing accretion, although oscillations are also found in sources without detected accretion-powered pulsations – for an extensive review see Watts (2012). The mechanism giving rise to the oscillations remains under debate, with some explanations preferring a broad but limited hot spot growing from the ignition point, while others involve the excitation of surface modes (Watts 2012; Galloway and Keek 2021). Observed pulse profiles during these bursts consist of broad sinusoidal peaks (e.g., Kini et al. 2023). 

D.4 Non-accreting Millisecond Pulsars 

The term “pulsar” was originally coined for the type of pulsating radio sources first detected by Jocelyn Bell in 1967 (Hewish et al. 1968; Hewish 1968), or an isolated neutron star producing pulsating radiation, which can span the whole electromagnetic spectrum. Some of these sources are detected in X-rays. These neutron stars have been formed very recently, by comparison with other X-ray pulsars. In some instances, the remnant of the associated supernova remnant may be visible, as in the Crab Pulsar and its Nebula. Their magnetic fields are strong and may exceed 10 12 G. Their ages, inferred from electromagnetic spin-down, range from thousands to of order a million years, after which they eventually reach a “death line,” where the electromagnetic emission turns o ff . Emission mechanisms are very di ff erent from the pulsars considered here. These young rotation-powered pulsars are mentioned mainly to distinguish them from a di ff erent group, the X-ray millisecond pulsars (MSPs). As their name implies, X-ray MSPs have spin periods in the millisecond range, achieved by recycling through accretion in binary systems. The spin-up to milliseconds requires 10 8 years or more. Although they resemble the younger pulsars just described in the sense that, with accretion spin-up having ceased, they now spin down by electromagnetic torques (but with much weaker magnetic fields, of order 10 8 G). Analogies between X-ray MSPs and younger isolated pulsars largely end there. The physics of these sources and their emission is once again very di ff erent from the accreting X-ray pulsars reviewed here. However, there are ways that the X-ray MSPs do have overlap with the topics in this review. One such overlap is that these are the two main instances where emission is produced su ffi ciently near the star surface that gravitational light bending is now routinely used in constructing theoretical light curves. In the MSP case, this is because the X-ray emission is primarily from hot spots directly on the neutron star surface, heated to X-ray temperatures 112 by particles accelerated towards the foot points of the B-field lines. In the accreting pulsars, the bulk of the emission produced in the accretion column models comes from within a few kilometers of the surface. Also, in both cases these comparatively low altitude emissions can be a ff ected by any complexity in the field configuration near the neutron star surface. In recent years several studies of isolated millisecond pulsar B-field and hot spot configura-tions have been published based on pulse profile observations by NICER , for instance for PSR J0030 +0451 (Bilous et al. 2019; Miller et al. 2019; Riley et al. 2019; Kalapotharakos et al. 2021) or PSR J0740 +6620 (Wol ff et al. 2021; Riley et al. 2021; Miller et al. 2021; Salmi et al. 2022) and such results are referred to as examples of the complexity of neutron star B-fields. Furthermore, the light curves are a ff ected by gravitational light bending because the emission is directly from the NS surface, in regions of strong gravitational fields. In some of the papers just cited, the light bending is exploited not only to derive theoretical light curves but also to constrain the equation of state for neutron stars. 

D.5 Magnetars 

The term “magnetar” was first used by Duncan and Thompson (1992) for very highly magne-tized neutron stars, B = 10 14 –10 15 G, formed in supernova explosions and invoked as possible gamma-ray burst sources, especially soft gamma-ray repeaters. In the original definition, the term implied a pulsed luminosity larger than the typical dipole spin-down luminosity, that is Lpulsed ≫ Iω ˙ω. Their high-energy activity is explained by the evolution and decay of an ultrastrong B-field, stressing and breaking the neutron star crust. This domain of processes associated with ultra-strong surface B-fields is the basic reason why magnetars are excluded in this review from consideration as part of the focus group. Reviews of these sources have been published, for instance, by Mereghetti (2011), Mereghetti et al. (2015), Kaspi and Beloborodov (2017), or Esposito et al. (2021). The periods of pulsating magnetars cluster in the range of 2–12 s and observed pulse profiles often show broad single peaks but with a variety of individual shapes(see, e.g., Dib and Kaspi 2014; Hu et al. 2019). Magnetar pulse profiles can show strong energy dependence with one pulse dominating at lower and another at higher energies (e.g., Ibrahim et al. 2024) and also a strong evolution with time, for example the change from a triple-peaked to a single-peaked profile during an outburst decay (Younes et al. 2022). Despite the very di ff erent mechanisms invoked to explain the bright X-ray emissions, the pulse profiles of magnetars show a similar phenomenology to those found in a wide range of accreting X-ray pulsars. This may be due to the energy release occurring near the surface of the neutron star. One might expect that the geometrical ingredients of light curve modeling to be nearly the same for magnetars, but with no requirement for the spectrum to be the same. 

D.6 White Dwarf Pulsators – Polars and Intermediate Polars 

White dwarfs accreting from a companion star (cataclysmic variables; CVs) can also show up as X-ray pulsators. The companions are Roche lobe filling stars on or near the main sequence or symbiotic stars where the mass donor is a late type giant. See Mukai (2017) for a general review. 113 These magnetic CVs are divided into two major subclasses: Intermediate Polars (Patterson 1994) 8, also known as DQ Herculis stars, which have typical B-field strengths of 10 5–7 G, and Polars (Cropper 1990), also called AM Her stars, with B-fields of 10 7–9 G do not possess accretion disks. Accretion onto white dwarfs in Polars occurs by accreted matter to stream directly to the magnetic poles, unlike Intermediate Polars which have truncated accretion disks and magnetically funnel matter onto their poles from the inner edge of the disks. The spin and the orbital period are synchronized for Polars whereas for Intermediate Polars the spin and orbital period are not synchronized.In the case of Polars, this magnetic synchronization requires a very strong stellar magnetic moment, ( BR 3)/2. While B is smaller than for neutron stars in the focus class, of order 10 7 G, R3 compensates to give moments comparable to magnetars. At the distance of the companion star, it is the magnetic moment that governs field strength. Modeling these systems follows lines analogous to models for accretion columns in the focus class. Equation 1 applies, evaluated for the white dwarf masses and radii. Roughly half of the emission, being produced at altitudes that are only a few percent of the stellar radius, goes into the polar cap and heats it to where it can become appreciable as black body emission, in UV or very soft X-rays. It is also possible to have a harder X-ray component by the inverse Compton mechanism. Thus, the accretion geometry is similar to accreting X-ray pulsars but emission mechanisms are distinctly di ff erent. In addition the much larger radii and lower masses of white dwarfs mean that light bending does not play a role in shaping the pulse profiles. In the case of Intermediate Polars, modulations at the beat frequency between spin and orbital period are frequently observed (Mukai 2017; Page and Shaw 2022, and references therein). This is another case where temporal e ff ects in CVs have neutron star analogs, but they do not impact understanding of light curves in the focus class.
